## Editor’s Preface.

---

The very important insights in this small, but nevertheless rich collection of the rarest knowledge <note>German: *Kenntnisse*; in this context, it refers to specialized or perhaps esoteric information</note> — were first shared with a deep-searching <term>deep-searching<note>The original German *tiefforschend* describes a friend who is a dedicated scholar or an investigator of profound, hidden truths.</note></term> friend: hence this all too free flow of ideas: and just as freely must everything

<summary>This page contains the beginning of the editor's preface, explaining that the following collection of rare knowledge was originally written as an informal exchange between friends.</summary>
<keywords>preface, rare knowledge, flow of ideas, scholarly correspondence</keywords>

be read; written not for the student,
but for the expert <term>expert<note>The original German *Kenner* refers to a connoisseur or someone with deep, specialized knowledge in a particular field.</note></term>.
He who now, in turn, gathers from this
collection, choosing from this colorful bouquet <note>German: *Strausse*; a metaphorical way to describe a diverse and beautiful selection of texts or ideas.</note>;
he will easily come to know and possess
the most magnificent things of this noble kind.

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>Following the Editor’s Preface, which established the intended expert audience, this page begins the main discourse. It transitions from the introductory metaphors to a foundational argument regarding the Jewish context of the New Testament.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>1</sig>

---

All New Testament authors, writing as <term>Jewish Christians<note>The original *Judenchristen* refers to the earliest followers of Jesus who were ethnically Jewish and continued to observe Jewish customs and law while developing early Christian theology.</note></term>, made use of Jewish idioms in their descriptions and development of Christian doctrines. Later Christian writers either failed to understand the true meaning of these expressions entirely, or they were unwilling to understand and accept them. It is likewise impossible to correctly grasp this meaning without consulting Jewish writings—not only the books of the Old Testament themselves, but also the commentaries on them provided by the Jewish people. These works, along with the so-called <term>oral traditions<note>Known as *Torah she-be-al-peh*, these were the interpretations and legal decisions passed down by word of mouth before being written down in the Mishnah and Talmud.</note></term>, were, at the time the Christian church emerged, among the

<vocab>Jewish Christians, idioms, Old Testament books, oral traditions, Christian church</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the author's primary thesis: that New Testament scripture is rooted in Jewish thought and idioms, which have been misunderstood by later Christian scholars who ignored Jewish commentaries and oral traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Jewish Christians, New Testament interpretation, Jewish idioms, oral tradition, biblical commentaries, early church history, Old Testament</keywords>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>2</page-num>

prevailing <term>Pharisaic<note>The Pharisees were a social movement and a school of thought in the Holy Land during the time of Second Temple Judaism. While often portrayed negatively in later traditions, they were the ancestors of Rabbinic Judaism and emphasized the "Oral Torah" alongside written scripture.</note></term> sect, held in equal esteem with them; and since the founder of Christianity, though he criticized the hypocritical lives of the Pharisees and warned against imitating them, nevertheless recognized their doctrines as correct and worthy of following ($^1$): so he, no less than the <term>Evangelists<note>The authors of the four Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note></term> and Apostles, attributed the same value to the oral traditions of the Jews as to their actual biblical scriptures.

The individual petitions in the **Lord’s Prayer** <note>The "Our Father" or *Vater Unser*.</note> are found in the prayers of the Jews, and they are generally regarded—even by later, orthodox Christian writers—as being borrowed from older Jewish prayers. ($^2$) „Our Father, who

<page-num>6</page-num>

if one knows the correct meaning of the Jewish idiom <note>original: "jüdische Redensart"</note> used here. In the wilderness, God gave the Israelites so much manna on the sixth day that it was also sufficient for the following day or for the Sabbath ($^6$); in the Jewish writings, it is therefore said that he who works on the sixth day shall receive the food he needs for the Sabbath. In this idiom, "sixth day" is intended to be synonymous with *today* and signifies *this world*; but "Sabbath" means as much as *tomorrow* and refers to *the world to come* <note>original: "die künftige Welt," referring to "Olam Ha-Ba" in Hebrew theology</note> ($^7$); therefore the prayer

Qui vero non labo-"</note> It is said that the nations shall appear before God at the final judgment and shall ask that God prescribe for them the Law to be observed anew, which they would then wish to keep. God will answer them: "O most foolish of men who are in the world; he who has worked on Friday <note>original Latin: "die Veneris"</note> (<gloss>Gloss: that is, in the present age</gloss>) shall have what he may eat on the Sabbath. This is a notoriously difficult word to translate; while usually rendered as "daily," scholars have long debated if it means "for the coming day" or "necessary for existence."</note> there stood a word which means both tomorrow and the future time ($^9$). 11. Targum Onkelos <note>An ancient Aramaic translation and commentary on the Torah</note> has here, *Jomaden* <note>original Aramaic: יומא דין</note>, on this day. since God created the day, He also

(¹⁰) Whoever has bread in his basket and says: "What shall I eat tomorrow?" belongs to those who possess little faith. *Sotah*, leaf 48, column 2. <note>original Latin: "Quicunque habet panem in canistro suo, et dicit: quid edam cras? est ex iis, qui exigua fide praediti sunt." The *Sotah* is a volume of the Babylonian Talmud.</note> He who has something to eat today and says: "What shall I eat tomorrow?" lacks faith, for He who created the day also created its food. *Tanchuma*, leaf 29. 4. <note>original Latin: "Qui habet, quod edat hodie, et dicit: quid edam cras? is caret fide, nam qui creavit diem, creavit quoque alimentum ejus." *Tanchuma* refers to a collection of Jewish homilies.</note> At the hour when Jeremiah said to the Israelites: "Why do you not devote yourselves to the Law <note>The Torah</note>?" they answered him: "On what shall we then live and be sustained?" He therefore brought out to them the jar of Manna and said (referencing Jeremiah 2:31), — that is, behold that with which your fathers, who devoted themselves to the Law, were fed. *Mekhilta*, leaf 20, 1 on Exodus 16:33. <note>The *Mekhilta* is a legal commentary on the book of Exodus. The "jar of manna" refers to the vessel kept in the Ark of the Covenant as a physical reminder of God's provision in the desert.</note>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 9 regarding trust in divine providence, moving from the example of the Manna in the desert to a statement attributed to Abraham. It concludes by beginning a transition into how the New Testament and the Talmud share imagery regarding the afterlife and punishment.</meta>

care for, and that one should leave worldly matters to God's own providence and care most for the future life. A statement by Abraham is cited: “I am flesh and blood; I am **today** here and **tomorrow** in the grave; I have done my part now or today; **let God do according to His will in His world** (**¹¹**);” where the expressions **today** and **tomorrow** are also explained as referring to this world and the future world. <note>This refers to the Jewish concepts of *Olam Ha-Zeh* (this world) and *Olam Ha-Ba* (the world to come), suggesting that man's labor belongs to this life, but the outcome belongs to God's eternal plan.</note>

The enemies of the Jews are threatened with terrible punishments by means of the idiom that **their worm shall not die**, which is also used in the New Testament writings. <note>This imagery appears in Mark 9:48, itself a reference to Isaiah 66:24, depicting eternal decay and suffering.</note> But its true meaning can only be grasped by means of the Talmud, where

Much has been written, and with much confusion, regarding what kind of transgression the **sin against the Holy Spirit** might be. <meta>This page continues the metaphor from page 22, completing the sentence regarding the "ignited light" used to find the "precious gem" of hidden knowledge. It further defines the hierarchy between the literal text and the mystical tradition.</meta>

—ignited light. The **literal sense** was regarded as nothing more than the extent to which it agreed with the explanations of the **Kabbalists**. These explanations were esteemed above all else and regarded as the essence of the true meaning of the Scripture, even when they obviously conflicted with the literal sense ($^{21}$). 

Whoever understood only the literal sense was compared to a pharmacist who prepares medicines <term>Arzneymittel: historical term for prepared pharmaceutical remedies</term> without knowing their effects; the **Kabbalists**, however, were compared to physicians who prescribe medicines and know exactly wherein their power consists.

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the "Athbash" cipher, demonstrating how the cryptic phrase "leb kamaj" in the Book of Jeremiah is actually a coded reference to the Chaldeans, proving that such mystical interpretations were integrated into the earliest translations of the Bible.</meta>

Hebrew alphabet is written from right to left and the other half back, from left to right: thus the last letter comes exactly under the first, the second-to-last comes under the second, and so forth. This secret mode of writing, which the **Kabbalists** <term>Kabbalists: Scholars and practitioners of Jewish mysticism who believe the Hebrew alphabet contains divine secrets.</term> call the *Athbash* alphabet <note>original: *Athbasch*. A substitution cipher where the first letter (Aleph) is replaced by the last (Tav), the second (Beth) by the second-to-last (Shin), hence the name A-Th-B-Sh.</note>, is applied in such a way that instead of the actual letters, those placed directly above or below them are used; and so, *Casdim* <note>Hebrew: כשדים (*Kasdim*), the Hebrew name for the Chaldean people.</note> arises instead of *leb kamaj* <note>Hebrew: לב קמי (*leb qamay*). In Jeremiah 51:1, this phrase literally means "the heart of those who rise up against me," but through the Athbash cipher, it transforms into "Kasdim" (Chaldeans).</note>. *Casdim* is, as is well known, the Hebrew name for the **Chaldeans** and corresponds to the name translated by the aforementioned **LXX** <note>The Septuagint, the earliest Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note> as "Chaldeans" <note>original Greek: Χαλδαίοι (*Chaldaioi*).</note>. That such a method of interpretation is ancient and was so generally accepted that it was considered among the primary principles of the Hebrew language is placed beyond all doubt by what follows.

In ancient Jewish prayers, a being named *Mazphaz* or *Mizfaz* <note>These names are the Athbash equivalents of the sacred four-letter name of God, YHVH.</note> is invoked, which is considered to be the name of a—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the sanctity of the divine name and the necessity of oral tradition in understanding Jewish mystical ciphers. It builds on the previous examples of letter substitution (like Athbash) to explain why these secrets cannot be learned from books alone.</meta>

Since it was considered a great sin to pronounce the name *Jehovah* <note>original: "Jehovah"</note>, one also avoided [giving] a person a name that [consisted] of the same let[ters]—

---

—[so that] no one can grasp it unless they have been instructed by others. If this is not done, one will fall headlong into error and idolatry. To make this clearer to the reader, I will explain it with an example. In a certain ancient prayer, we find the name *Matzpitz* <term>מצפץ</term> <note>original Hebrew: "מצפץ"</note>, which an unskilled person might believe to be the name of some angel; however, it is actually a synonym for the four-lettered name <note>The Tetragrammaton, the holy name of God usually written as YHWH</note>, with the letters exchanged according to the *Athbash* <term>אתבּשׁ</term> <note>A cipher where the first letter of the Hebrew alphabet is replaced by the last, the second by the second-to-last, and so on.</note> alphabet. Likewise, the phrase *Kuzu Bemuchzasz Kuzu* <term>כוזו במכ״סז כוזו</term> <note>original: "כוזו במכ״סז כוזו"</note> has the same meaning as *Jehovah Elohenu Jehovah* <term>יהוה אלהינו יהוה</term> <note>meaning "The Lord our God, the Lord"</note> when each individual letter is exchanged for the following letter in the alphabetical sequence—namely, the letter *Yod* <term>י</term> is replaced with *Kaph* <term>כ</term>, and *He* <term>ה</term> with *Vav* <term>ו</term> <note>This is known as the "Achas Beta" or "AvGad" cipher, a simple Caesar-style shift.</note>. 

From this it is evident that no one is permitted to handle Kabbalistic mysteries based on their own conjecture or effort, unless they have been taught by someone else who is already knowledgeable in Kabbalistic matters. For it is not possible to learn the true method of this science from any writing; therefore, it is called *Kabbalah* <note>The word Kabbalah comes from the Hebrew root meaning "to receive," emphasizing that it is a tradition received from a teacher.</note>, and its nature corresponds to its name. 

Rabbi Joseph Albo <note>A famous 15th-century Jewish philosopher</note>, Treatise II, Chapter 28. Odhelius, *The Two-Fronted Synagogue*, page 89. Buxtorf, *Talmudic Lexicon*, page 249.

<meta>This page continues the translation of Saint Jerome’s commentary on the Book of Jeremiah, specifically providing the technical explanation of the Atbash cipher and the justification for using such codes in sacred texts to avoid political persecution.</meta>

### Here I have a reliable guide for the discovery of the true meaning

---

...to the very last letter in order, that is, Alpha, Beta, and so on, up to Omega; and again, for the sake of helping children memorize, we are accustomed to reverse the order of the reading and mix the last letters with the first, so that we say Alpha, Omega, Beta, Psi <note>original: "Pfi", likely referring to the letter Phi in a reversed pairing sequence</note>; so it is also among the Hebrews: the first is Aleph, the second is Beth, the third is Gimel, up to the twenty-second and final letter, Thau, for which the second to last is Sin. Therefore, we read them as pairs: Aleph with Thau, and Beth with Sin. And when we come to the middle of the alphabet at the letter Lamed, it meets the letter Caph <note>In the <term>Atbash</term> cipher, letters from the beginning of the alphabet are swapped with letters from the end. Aleph (1st) swaps with Tav (22nd), Beth (2nd) with Shin (21st), and so on, meeting in the middle at the 11th and 12th letters, Caph and Lamed.</note>. And just as, if we read in the normal way, we read *Babel*; so by changing the order, we read *Sesach* <note>original: "Sesach", the biblical name Sheshach found in Jeremiah 25:26 and 51:41.</note>. And I believe that the name *Babel* was wisely hidden by the Holy Prophet [Jeremiah], lest he openly incite the rage of those against him who were besieging *Jerusalem* and were just about to capture it. We read that the Apostle [Paul] did the same thing regarding the *Roman Empire* when writing about the *Antichrist*: "Do you not remember that when I was still with you, I told you these things? And now you know what is restraining him, so that he may be revealed..." <note>A reference to 2 Thessalonians 2:5-6. Jerome argues that Paul used the vague phrase "that which restrains" as a code for the Roman Empire to avoid charges of sedition or inciting persecution.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the technical explanation of the Atbash cipher, showing how "Babel" becomes "Sheshach." It explains that both Jeremiah and the Apostle Paul used such cryptographic methods to protect themselves and their followers from the ruling political powers.</summary>

For if he had wanted to speak openly, he would have foolishly incited persecutions against the Christians and the rage of the Church then coming into being. Jerome’s Commentary, Book V on Jeremiah, Chapter XXV. Works Vol. V, p. 326.

<meta>This page moves from the general introduction of Jewish mysticism into the specific definitions of the "Sephirot." It follows the author's previous assertion that understanding these divine emanations is the essential key to decoding the symbolic language of the Book of Revelation.</meta>

Sephiroth or Sphiroth is the plural of *Sephira* or *Sphirah*, which in the <term>Chaldean: a historical term used by scholars to refer to the Aramaic language</term> dialect—<note>original: "Sfar, Sifra"</note>—means calculation, number, or determination. According to the explanation of some Jewish scholars, it also signifies brilliance and clarity <note>referring to the Hebrew root for 'sapphire,' implying a radiant light</note>, as well as a boundary or limitation. 

The <term>Kabbalists: practitioners of a mystical tradition seeking to understand the relationship between the infinite God and the finite universe</term> attributed seven properties to God in regard to His effects and actions in the world, through which these actions of His are carried out. However, when they rise in their reflection from God's manifestations in the world to His essence itself, they imagine this essence through three even higher properties. These three essential properties of God and His seven executive properties are known together as the ten *Sephiroth*, *Sephirs*, or digits; they are the ten numbers, the ten clarities, or the ten boundaries where all human conceptual thought must stand still, and which no intellect is able to surpass.

From the observation of the world and of nature, humans first

<meta>This page completes the sentence fragment from page 42 ("The Greek phi-") and explains the cosmological division of the universe according to ancient Chaldean theology.</meta>

losophers refer to the oldest theology of the Chaldeans <note>The Chaldeans were an ancient Mesopotamian people renowned in the ancient world for their advanced systems of astronomy, mathematics, and mysticism.</note> and say that everything associated with it was compared to numbers and actually understood under the **tenfold number** <note>German: *Zehnzahl*. In many ancient systems, ten was considered the "perfect number" or the limit of numerical progression, encompassing all other numbers within it.</note>, as the limit of all counting. Everything that received the name Nature was divided into three categories: the first was *Eternal*, and by this, God was understood, as *One* and *Good*, as the *First*, the *Beginning* of all things, and *God the Father*. Of the second or middle category, it was said that it had a beginning but would never have an end; to this were reckoned spirits, human souls, and thoughts, which were considered divine beings directly under God the Father. The third category contained that which consisted of substance or *matter* and was divided into *seven worlds* <note>Likely referring to the seven planetary spheres (Moon, Mercury, Venus, Sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn) which were thought to make up the visible universe.</note>, whose center was found in the first, supernatural, incorporeal *light*, the image of the depth of God the *Father* <note>The "depth" (German: *Tiefe*) refers to the "Bythos," a term used in ancient mystical philosophy to describe the unfathomable, original source of the Divine.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient Chaldean theology, specifically detailing the nature of the divine hierarchy and the concept of a "Triune" deity.</meta>

Furthermore, an attempt was made to express this view in such a way that the highest and first *beginning*, the *cause* of all things, was designated as *triune* <note>German: *Dreyeinig*. While usually a Christian term, the author is applying it here to ancient "pagan" philosophies that also envisioned a threefold divine nature.</note>, consisting of three *persons* who constituted *one* single divine *essence*; these persons were the *Unity*, or *God the Father*; the *Duality*, the *Word* <note>Often referred to in philosophy as the *Logos*, the divine reason or ordering principle.</note> or the *Intellect*; and the *Spirit* or the *Soul*. This duality was contained within the unity and formed the second and third persons within the deity. The first person had created everything through the second, to whom it had also transferred the governance over its created works; by means of the duality, everything was born, produced, and brought to life. This supreme unity was considered so incapable of being shared <note>original: *unmittheilbar*</note> ($^{28}$), that it was said to exist neither in itself nor in anything else—

<meta>This page continues the critique of the Swedish Trial Bible's translation of Genesis 1:2, specifically focusing on how the Swedish version fails to capture the linguistic parallels in the Hebrew text describing the primordial state of the world.</meta>

"darkness over wide-spread water."
The two identical constructions "over the face of the deep" <note>original: "al pne thom"</note> and "over the face of the waters" <note>original: "al pne hamajim"</note> have been given a contradictory meaning in the Swedish Trial Translation <note>The *Provbibel* of 1774</note>, even though they are a continuation of one and the same thought. According to common usage, these different words all express the same complete meaning. The words "emptiness," "void," "darkness," and "spirit of God" <note>original: "thohu, bhohu, chofchaek, ruach elohim"</note>—Desolation, Emptiness, Darkness, and a wide-spread expanse of wind—correspond here to one another. They are all used to compare the first horrific condition of the earth with the nature of an immeasurable, completely dark airspace, in which winds hovered over an equally dark, limitless expanse of water; for this reason, it was believed that the earth consisted of these elements at its very beginning. *Deep* <note>original: "Thom," referring to the Hebrew *Tehom*</note> signifies a great and limitless accumulation of wa- <note>The word is cut off: "Wasser" (water)</note>

Just as Mount Bashan—since it was hostile toward God’s people—was called "God’s mountain" for no other reason than because it was a great, formidable mountain, and this designation thus signifies nothing more than a high mountain; just so, in the **Creation Story** <note>original: Schöpfungsge- ſchichte</note>, *God’s wind* or *spirit over the waters* is nothing other than a widespread wind over the waters, and corresponds to the preceding phrase: *darkness over wide-spread water*. <meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the Genesis creation narrative and the use of Hebrew superlatives. of His righteousness, is compared with a boundless water, in order to give a concept of its infinity.

Translating the word *jom* <note>original: *jom* (יוֹם)</note> as *full day* is more correct than simply as *day*. It signifies both a *full day* as well as a *day*, as well as time in general. Here, one imagines that the first form of the Earth, before the emergence of light, resembled a vast, dark space of water and air—a space intended for light—and that only then did God create light so that it might alternate with the darkness. This light, which then came into being, is said to have lasted until evening, or until the return of darkness, which then reigned until the next morning when the light appeared again. Therefore, the reckoning begins with the light that has now appeared for the first time, which is why it says here:

> “And God called the light
> “Day; but the darkness he called Night.”

Nameless, since the light that had appeared vanished again, there began anew the dark—

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the Genesis creation account, specifically interpreting the transition from the first darkness to the sequence of the first "full day" (Volltag). It picks up from the previous page's catchword "dark-" (tene-).</meta>

darkness, which received the name Night; when this ceased again, it became morning; for this reason, the narrative continues, and reads exactly according to these words: “And it was evening, and it was “morning—a full day; or: the first “full day;” <note>The author uses the term "Volltag" to distinguish a complete 24-hour cycle of day and night from the daylight portion of a day.</note> not a day; for both the first day and the first night had now passed. Here, therefore, evening precedes morning because the calculation goes from the time when the light had its first beginning until the time when it appeared for the second time. This second appearance of light is what is called morning here, and the evening precedes it. The correct translation is therefore the one already cited: that the first full day, or the first division of time, consisted of day and night.

In Latin, the sense can be expressed in the following way:

> The heavens, which exist, and the earth which exists, were first created by God. The earth was then a thing formless and empty, namely dark—
> <note>original: "Coeli, qui sunt, et quae existit terra, a Deo primum creati sunt. Terra tum fuit res informis et inanis, nempe tene-"</note>

<summary>This page argues that the biblical "day" in the creation story should be understood as a "full day" (Volltag) encompassing both light and darkness, explaining why evening is mentioned before morning. It concludes with a Latin rendering of the opening of Genesis.</summary>

<keywords>Genesis, creation narrative, full day, Volltag, morning and evening, biblical philology, Latin translation, darkness and light</keywords>

<meta>This page completes the Latin translation of the Genesis opening begun on page 74. The word fragment "tene-" from the previous page is completed here as "tenebrae" (darkness). It transitions from a theological translation to an astronomical explanation of the creation week.</meta>

...ness over the vast mass of waters, and an immense force of winds moving itself over those waters. When indeed God said, "Let there be light," there was light; and God saw that this very light was good, wherefore God brought it about that this light and that darkness should alternate; and God called that light Day, and he called that darkness Night; thus there was evening (the day having ended), and there was morning (the night having ended), the first full day. <note>original: "...brae super vastam aquarum molem... prima nychthemeri." The Latin text uses the term "nychthemeri," derived from Greek, to signify a complete 24-hour period of day and night.</note>

The story of creation <note>German: *Schöpfungsgeschichte*</note> is then continued until the seventh day, where it ends, and the day is sanctified. The first measurement of time was thus an astronomical day, and the one following that was constituted by every seventh day. If one investigates the natural cause of this, it becomes quite clear. Following the first division of time by means of day and night, which was attributed to the sun, there followed next that division which occurs through the moon, which changes its appearance every seventh day. The sun shows its

<meta>This page continues the transition from the biblical creation narrative to a naturalistic and philosophical explanation of time. It specifically picks up from the previous page's discussion of the sun and moon as the primary instruments for measuring days and weeks.</meta>

...determination of time during the day, and the moon by night. The story of creation <note>German: *Schöpfungsgeschichte*</note> therefore says that the sun rules the day and the moon the night; the sun brought about the shortest division of time within 24 hours, while the moon brought about a longer division within 7 days.

The teachings of the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of the Greek philosopher Pythagoras, who believed numbers were the fundamental reality of the universe</note>, which were borrowed from the Orient <note>German: *Morgenland*, literally "morning-land"</note> and from Egypt, held that the Creator rules the lower world through the sun, the moon, and the five planets; they held that the moon, which was the seventh of these <note>In ancient geocentric astronomy, the "seven planets" included the Sun, the Moon, Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>, was ruled by the "number seven." This number seven was also considered the cause of its orbital period, which is why the moon spends seven days from the beginning of its appearance until its first half <note>The first quarter phase</note>, and again seven days until its fullness; thereafter another seven days until it appears as a half-moon again, and finally seven days until it becomes completely invisible once more. This entire orbital period was called a year <note>This likely refers to a "lunar month" being treated as a complete cycle or "year" of the moon's phases</note>, and the

<term>Zeitbestimmung</term> (Determination of time), <term>Schöpfungsgeschichte</term> (Story of creation), <term>Sonne</term> (Sun), <term>Mond</term> (Moon), <term>Pythagoräer</term> (Pythagoreans), <term>Morgenland</term> (The Orient/East), <term>Aegypten</term> (Egypt), <term>Planeten</term> (Planets), <term>Siebenzahl</term> (The number seven/septenary), <term>Umlaufszeit</term> (Orbital period or cycle)

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the moon’s role in early human history, moving from the astronomical cycle of seven days discussed on the previous page to a broader linguistic and cultural analysis of how the moon defined our concept of time and years.</meta>

Lunar motion was, after day and night, the first guide to the calculation of time. The name of the moon in the Ethiopian dialect is *Warh*, in Chaldean <note>An ancient Semitic language of Mesopotamia</note> *Irahh*, *Iarhha*, *Jarcha*, in Syrian *Jarcho*, in Hebrew *Jareahh*, *Järah*, or *Järach*, which also signifies the orbital period of the moon, or a short year <note>A "short year" here refers to a lunar month being viewed as a complete cycle</note>; from these are derived the Arabic *Warehha* and *Aracha*, meaning to compose a calculation of time, and *Århah* or *Orchah*, a certain specific time or annual account. In Latin, this corresponds to *Aera* <note>meaning an era or fixed point in time</note>, with which the German *year* <note>original: *Jahr*</note> and the Swedish *År* correspond.

The phase of the moon thus provided the first guidance for measuring time in intervals of every seven days, and likewise gave rise to the sanctity of the number seven and the seventh day. It was believed that the sun, the moon, and the five planets known since antiquity <note>These five planets—Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn—along with the sun and moon, made up the seven "wandering stars" of the ancient world</note> had an influence on the Earth and the events occurring upon it.

<summary>This page explores the etymological roots of the word "moon" across several ancient languages, demonstrating how the lunar cycle established the seven-day week and the fundamental concept of a "year" or "era." It also introduces the ancient belief in the astrological influence of the seven celestial bodies on Earthly affairs.</summary>
<keywords>lunar motion, etymology, time-keeping, seven-day week, number seven, planets, astrology, ancient languages</keywords>

($^{54}$) <note>original Latin: "Sicut lunaris annus menſis eſt, quia Luna paulo minus quam menſem in Zodiaci circumitione conſumit, ita ſolis annus hoc dierum numero colligendus eſt, quem peragit, dum ad id ſignum ſe denuo vertit, ex quo digreſſus eſt. <meta>This page continues the explanation of how the Egyptian method of counting hours led to the naming of the days of the week, following the mathematical logic established on page 88. It also shifts to discussing the cultural symbolism of celestial bodies and the number seven in Egypt and Persia.</meta>

assigns the first hour to Saturn, as the first planet, the second to Jupiter, and so forth, then the first hour of the following day falls to the Sun; the first hour of the third day to the Moon, and so forth. From this, the first day received the name Saturday <note>original: "Saturnstag"</note>, the second the name Sunday <note>original: "Sonnentag"</note>, and the third the name Monday <note>original: "Mondstag"</note>, and so forth (⁸²) <note>This "planetary hour" system works because 24 (hours in a day) divided by 7 (planets) leaves a remainder of 3. Thus, if the first hour of Day 1 is Saturn, the first hour of Day 2 is three planets further in the sequence, which is the Sun.</note>. The Egyptians compared the Sun to a King and to the right eye; the Moon to a Queen and to the left eye; the five planets to a bodyguard of the King and Queen, and the remaining stars to the common people (⁸³). Among the ancient Persians, the number of the most prominent counselors was seven (⁸⁴).

Capitoline Jupiter was divided into three chambers: in the middle was that of Jupiter, to the right that of Minerva, and to the left that of Juno ($^{88}$). The sacred institutions of the Jews are similar to those of the other Eastern <note>original German: *morgenländischen*, literally "morning-land"</note> peoples, and share the same origin with them. <note>This passage describes the medieval cosmological model of "Separate Intelligences." In this view, God (the First Cause) emanates a series of Intellects, each of which governs a celestial sphere—from the outermost stars down through the known planets to the Moon. Each sphere is treated as a living entity possessing both a "soul" (the driver of motion) and a "body" (the physical orb).</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jewish cosmology and philosophy, specifically linking the "Active Intellect" of the Aristotelian tradition (discussed on the previous page) to the tenth Sephirah of the Kabbalistic tradition.</meta>

Exactly in the same way, the Kabbalists <note>Jewish mystics who studied the inner meanings of the Torah</note> or the wise men of the Jews formed their concepts of God. Just as the tenth Intellect, in relation to the ten orbits of the celestial bodies, is called the active *Intellect*; so too is this name attributed to the tenth <term>Sephirah</term> <note>One of the ten attributes or emanations of God in Kabbalistic thought</note>, or the tenth of God's attributes, and it is said to have been created by God on the Sabbath (112): namely in the same sense as it was said of God that He completed His works on the seventh day or on the Sabbath, and how the actions of God in the world were ascribed to seven particular attributes, of which the seventh was made the dwelling place of God, from which all His actions proceeded.

The orbits of the celestial bodies are also compared to the number of fingers on human hands. The

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the astronomical significance of the Jewish Temple symbols, specifically completing the sentence about the twelve loaves of bread representing the months of the year.</meta>

of the year, which, along with much else, shows that the foundation for the institutions of the Jews must be sought much further back than among the people themselves. The Jews call the seven *planets servants of God*; they give the same name to the seven executive *attributes of God*, that is, God himself, considered according to his effects; the seven lamps on the lampstand <note>the Menorah</note> were an image of the seven *planets*; however, they were also an image of the seven executive *attributes of God*, that is, of God revealed in the world through his works and deeds.

The ten *Sephiroth* <term>the ten attributes or emanations in Jewish Kabbalah through which the Infinite reveals itself</term>, it is said, proceed from infinity, which was expressed with the two words *En Soph* <note>original: "En Soph," more commonly written as "Ein Sof," meaning "without end" or "the Infinite"</note>, that is, without end, no end, in order to communicate power and blessing from there to the lower world. Concerning this Infinite, it was not permitted to utter the slightest thing; all effects proceeding from it were understood under the name *Jehovah*.

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Sephiroth and the Infinite (Ein Sof), specifically addressing how the various emanations relate to each other and the etymological origins of the term "Sephirah."</meta>

and in that same essence, all ten Sephiroth <term>the ten attributes or emanations in Jewish Kabbalah through which the Infinite reveals itself</term> united into one *Unity* ($^{148}$).

---

($^{148}$) <note>original Latin: "Ex Infinito illo (En soph)..."</note> From that Infinite One (*En Soph* <note>more commonly written as "Ein Sof," meaning "without end" or "the Infinite"</note>) proceed all ten Sephiroth to draw down influence and blessing into the lower world. That very portion which we grasp with our intellect is called a *Sephirah*. Indeed, the interpretation of the word *Sephirah* stems from the meaning of *Sippur* <note>Hebrew: "telling" or "narration"</note> (that is, an account), because one is permitted to tell of them, whereas concerning the *En Soph*, as we have stated, it is not permitted to speak at all. Or we can also derive the etymology of the word *Sephirah* from purity and radiance, just as it is said in Exodus 24:10: "And under his feet was something like a paved work of sapphire stone." <note>The Hebrew words for "sapphire" (Sappir) and "Sephirah" share the same linguistic root, implying clarity or brilliance</note>. Or finally, it is permitted to say that the word *Sephirah* comes from the meaning of an extremity or the boundary of a thing, as when our Sages of blessed memory <note>a traditional Jewish honorific for deceased scholars</note> say: "a city situated on the border," or the furthest shore (*Sphar* <note>Hebrew: "frontier" or "coast"</note>). <meta>This page continues the discussion of the hidden nature of the Divine and how the ten Sephiroth (divine emanations) relate to the Infinite. It specifically addresses the concept of God's essence as "Nothingness" in the context of human understanding.</meta>

The essence of God, hidden from all human reason, which is designated under the name *Infinity* by the two words *En Soph* <note>more commonly written as "Ein Sof," meaning "without end"</note>, is also referred to sometimes simply by the word *En* <note>Hebrew: "Ayin," meaning "Nothing"</note> and sometimes by *Blimah* <note>meaning "nothingness" or "without anything," often referencing Job 26:7</note>. Both signify the same thing, namely *Nothing*; the sense is that the essence of God is, for all human inquiry, as if it were a *Nothing*. His attributes and effects are the only things that can, in part, be recognized through the contemplation of the created world. *En Soph* is regarded partly as being exalted above the ten Sephiroth, and partly as the first of the Sephiroth ($^{149}$). ...ten Sephiroth <term>the ten divine attributes or emanations through which the Infinite reveals itself</term> are arranged in what is known as a Kabbalistic Tree. For this reason, sometimes *En Soph* <note>original: "En Soph," from the Hebrew *Ein Sof*, meaning "the Infinite" or "Without End"</note>—or Infinity—is placed above this figure to indicate that God’s attributes originate from it. <meta>This page continues the discussion from page 153 regarding the origins of symbolic representation. It completes the thought that before the invention of formal writing, concepts were depicted through natural imagery.</meta>

[invented writ]ing, they designated thoughts and concepts by drawing animals whose characteristics were intended to make the corresponding concepts recognizable. When the ancient Egyptians wanted to indicate intellect, memory, power, and art, they drew a human being, because humans possess these qualities to the most perfect degree among all living creatures ($^{151}$). God, who carried out the work of creation, was called the creative intellect ($^{152}$), and the concept of this intellect was therefore also presented under the image of a human being; this is why God is described as a man and with human characteristics. The Brahma <note>The creator god in Hinduism</note> of the Indians is sometimes described as the highest being, who [prepares] the raw material for—

($^{151}$) <note>original: "Intellectum, memoriam, potentiam et artem Homo significans ab eis in Sacris insculpitur."</note> A human being, signifying intellect, memory, power, and art, is carved by them into sacred objects. Clement of Alexandria <note>A Christian theologian and philosopher (c. 150 – c. 215 AD) who often wrote about the relationship between Greek philosophy and religion</note>, *Stromata* Book V, p. 567.

($^{152}$) <note>original: "Opifex Intellectus"</note> The Creative Intellect or the Craftsman-Mind.

<meta>This page continues the explanation of Gematria (calculation-based Kabbalah) from the previous page, specifically detailing how the name Jehovah is numerically linked to other significant concepts.</meta>

culation-Kabbalah <term>Calculation-Kabbalah: a translation of the German "rechnungs-Kabbala," referring to Gematria, a system where the numerical values of letters are summed to find hidden connections between words</term>, words and sentences whose letters, when calculated, result in the same numbers or sums as the letters in the name *Jehovah* are computed; such words and sentences are then regarded as being contained within the name *Jehovah* itself and constitute what is known as the "explained name." In this manner, the name *Jehovah* is sometimes calculated so that it forms a phrase consisting of twelve letters. For this reason, the number twelve, which designates the twelve Jewish tribes, is also viewed as a designation for *Jehovah*. Thus, He becomes one and the same with the Jews, and *Jehovah* is one with His congregation. The twelve letters that are considered the "explained name" are said to contain this meaning: *Father, Son, and Holy Spirit* ($^{159}$) <note>This is a characteristic interpretation of "Christian Kabbalah," where scholars used Jewish mystical methods to find evidence of the Christian Trinity within Hebrew names and scriptures.</note>.

---

($^{159}$) Johannes Drusius, *Tetragrammaton, or concerning the Name Jod, He [Vav, He]* <note>original: *J. Druſii Tetragrammaton ſive de Nomine Jod, He — —*</note>, Chapter 2.
Pietro Galatino, *On the Secrets of Universal Truth* <note>original: *Galatinus, de Arcanis catholicae veritatis*</note>, Book II, Chapter 17.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jewish theology, specifically how concepts (Begriffe) of God’s essence and effects were categorized, following the previous page's explanation of why Jesus refused the title "good" based on these distinctions.</meta>

concepts regarding the essence and the
effects of God were diverse.
The Jewish doctrine of the **immortality of souls** was the same as the Pythagorean and ancient **Oriental** <note>literally "morning-land-ish," referring to the ancient Near East</note> doctrine: that souls were parts belonging to God’s own **essence** <note>German: *Wesen*; the fundamental nature of a being</note> and, as it were, had emanated from the same; these parts revealed themselves in the world, and afterwards, upon completion of their duty, returned back to God and were united with His own essence. The concepts of God and the division of His attributes according to His essence and His activities in the world were connected with the doctrine of souls, in that these were effects of God’s essence; for one ascended from the lower effects to the higher, and from there to the essence of God Himself, and all of this taken together constituted the concept of God. God, viewed according to His essence, was incomprehensible and hidden; but His...

One imagined God’s **essence** <note>German: *Wesen*; the fundamental, internal nature of God</note> to be above all effects; but the **attribute**, or—which means the same—the **person** of the same, which as it were first gave rise to the effect or **revelation** in the world, was that which the Jews designated with the name **Jehovah**, **Mediator**, **Father in Heaven**, and other titles. This **essential attribute** of God could not, as something essential, act directly within the world; for the essential attributes of God were considered too exalted for that; but the attributes of God that *could* act in the world were of a lower order, and these were employed by **Jehovah** to carry out His effects among the Jews. <meta>This page continues the discussion of God’s attributes and the divine name, moving from the concept of God’s "executive council" to the specific Kabbalistic hierarchy of the Sephirot (divine emanations). his upper Shekhinah <term>Shekhinah: the dwelling or settling divine presence of God</term> or heavenly dwelling was the third of God’s essential attributes, which was called *Understanding* <term>Understanding: likely referring to the Sephirah *Binah*, associated with the processing of divine energy</term>; for the highest essential attribute of God, which was called *Crown* <term>Crown: the Sephirah *Keter*, the highest point of emanation</term>, was considered just as inactive <note>German: *unwirksam*; meaning here that it is beyond human interaction or effect</note> as that which was called *En Soph* <term>En Soph: from the Hebrew *Ein Sof*, meaning "The Infinite," the unknowable essence of God prior to self-manifestation</term>, which is why these names often mean one and the same thing; and since the name *Jehovah* was an image of this incomprehensible essence of God, and therefore was not permitted to be pronounced, the *Crown* also signifies the same as this name, which is why the saying according to *Hillel* <note>Hillel the Elder was a famous Jewish sage and scholar of the 1st century BCE</note> is cited: *he who uses the crown, passes away, or dies*, by which he meant that he who pronounced the name of *Jehovah* could not remain among the living ($^{192}$). God, when considered in His essence, could not act upon the earth. The second person in the essence of God <note>This likely refers to the "Logos" or Word, a concept in Hellenistic Jewish philosophy (such as that of Philo of Alexandria) where a secondary divine principle mediates between the transcendent God and the material world</note> was the active person according to the teachings of the Jews; this person revealed himself as active in the world through Jesus, and Jesus was compared with God just as a ray of sunlight is compared with the sun itself ($^{198}$). One of the oldest Christian teachers <note>Tertullian (c. 155–220 AD), an early Christian author from Carthage who was the first to use the Latin word *trinitas* (trinity)</note> expresses himself thus:

> ($^{198}$) God of God, as a substantive entity, will not be God himself, but is God in so far as he is from the substance of God himself, which is also a substantive entity and a certain portion of the whole. I will not speak of "Gods" at all, nor of "Lords"; but I will follow the Apostle <note>the Apostle Paul</note>, so that if the Father and the Son are to be named together, I shall call the Father "God" and name Jesus Christ "Lord"; yet Christ alone I can call "God," as did the same Apostle in Romans 9. For I shall also call a ray of the sun "the sun" when it is by itself, but when naming the sun to which the ray belongs, I will not immediately call the ray "the sun" as well. <note>original Latin: "Deus Dei, tanquam substantiva res... Tertullianus adversus Praxeam 13, 26. Cfr. Epist. ad Hebr. 1, 3." This passage is from Tertullian’s polemic "Against Praxeas," which argued against the idea that the Father and Son were the exact same person.</note>

These lower attributes converged solely within the last or seventh one, and found in it their residence, their resting place, and their dwelling, from which God’s effects and activities in the world proceeded.

(210) <note>original Latin: "Videtur fuisse aliquis praeter Dominum..."</note> It appears there was someone besides the Lord who spoke with Moses as a mediator; however, they <note>Referring to the Jewish Sages.</note> respond that this mediator was **Metatron** <note>An angel in Jewish angelology who often serves as the celestial scribe or the "lesser YHWH."</note>, whose name is like the name of his Lord, and who is the **Prince of the Faces** (or persons, <term>panim</term> <note>The Hebrew word for "faces," often used to denote the Presence of God.</note>); he was not a common messenger like those of the other Prophets, who were addressed by various angels and intermediaries. **Rabbi Joseph Albo** <note>A 15th-century Spanish-Jewish philosopher.</note>, *Sermon II*, Chapter 28, folio 54. This speech addressed to Moses is attributed to the visible **Majesty of God**, which is also called the **Shekinah** <note>A term for the divine presence or "dwelling" of God on earth.</note>. **Zohar** <note>The primary text of Jewish mysticism (Kabbalah).</note>, Part II, folio 52, column 1. That supreme spiritual **Glory of Jehovah** denotes that first **Effect**, produced (or begotten) by the Blessed One Himself. For this is the **Beginning** (<term>thechillah</term>) of His ways, and the **First Principle** (<term>reshith</term>) of His creatures; and this is the very thing which is called, in the style of our Sages, the **She-** <note>The text breaks off at the beginning of the word "Shekinah."</note>

<summary>This page discusses the identity of the divine mediator who spoke with Moses, identifying him as Metatron or the Shekinah, and equating this figure with the "first effect" or "beginning" of God's creation.</summary>

God’s perfections are viewed as **pillars** upon which the whole rests and by which it is sustained; God’s grace illuminates—

The first degree, which is usually called the **Crown** <note>The Sephirah *Keter*, the highest and most unknowable emanation</note>, is also called the **Root**, specifically when all ten **Sephiroth** <term>Sephiroth: the ten divine attributes or emanations in Kabbalistic thought through which the Infinite reveals Himself</term> are given the collective name of the **Tree**. ---

($^{219}$) A tree has roots, and from the root rises the **offshoot** <note>original Latin: *germen*, also meaning sprout or bud</note>, and from the offshoot come the **branches**. There are three levels: **Root**, **Offshoot**, and **Branches**, and all of this constitutes a single tree. The only difference between these levels is that of the **hidden** and the **manifest**. The root, which is hidden, reveals its influence in the offshoot and unites itself with it; the offshoot, in turn, manifests its influence in the branches and unites itself with those very branches that sprout from it. In short, all things adhere to and unite themselves with the root itself; for if the offshoot were not the influence of the root, all the branches would dry up. For this reason, this tree is called **one**. 

— The **Highest Crown** <note>original: *Corona summa*, referring to Kether, the first Sephirah</note>, which is the mystery of the center, is the hidden root itself. The three **superior minds** <note>referring to the triad of Kether (Crown), Chokhmah (Wisdom), and Binah (Understanding)</note> are the offshoot, which unite themselves in the center, which is their root. The seven **Sephiroth**, however, which are the branches, unite themselves with the offshoot (which represents the minds), and all unite in the center. This center is the root within the mystery of the **radical and essential name** <note>referring to the Tetragrammaton, the four-letter divine name</note>, which flows into all and unites all through its influence. 

*The Book of Formation* <note>original: *Sephaer Jezirah* (Sefer Yetzirah), an ancient foundational text of Jewish mysticism</note>. 
From **Athanasius Kircher’s** *The Egyptian Oedipus* <note>original: *Oedipus Aegyptiacus*, a 17th-century work of universal symbols and syncretism</note>, Vol. II, p. 297.

It describes the Highest Crown as the hidden root that sustains the "minds" (offshoots) and the lower attributes (branches).</summary>

For the celebration of the Sabbath among the Jews, joyful abundance in eating and drinking was also required, and on every Sabbath, three meals had to be held. In the **kabbalistic** <term>kabbalistic: relating to the Jewish mystical tradition (Kabbalah) that seeks to uncover hidden meanings in the Torah</term> sense, these meals designated **Jehovah** according to the mystery that He is *One*, and that His name is *One* in Three and in Seven. The three Sabbath meals referred to the three essential **Sephiroth** <term>Sephiroth: the ten emanations or attributes in Kabbalah through which the Infinite reveals itself</term>, and the Sabbath, signifying Seven, referred to the seven executing Sephiroth. For this reason, the Sabbath with its three meals was considered an image of God, both in His essence and His work—

---

( 2 2 5 ) And in the little word *az* <note>original: *az*, אז, meaning 'then'</note>, there is an indication of the ten Sephiroth; for א <note>the letter Aleph, numerically 1</note> encompasses the three superior ones and ז <note>the letter Zayin, numerically 7</note> the seven extremities. And our Rabbis say of the word *az*: *“Come and let us praise that Unique One, who is carried above the seven.”* <note>original Latin: *venite et laudemus illum Unicum, qui invehitur ſuper ſeptem*</note> Rabbi Menachem of Recanati <note>a prominent 13th-14th century Italian kabbalist</note>, *On the Law* <note>original Latin: *in Leg.* (in Lege), referring to his commentary on the Torah</note>, folio 53, column 2.

...of that Name (that is, of God). And therefore these blessings will rest upon his head, and they will fulfill his will, just as he himself carries out that exalted will. For he who worships with the proper intention is the one who unites that Name with its perfections; and thus he bestows strength upon it, according to the mystery of the words in Psalm 60:12: "In God we shall do valiantly," and Psalm 68:34: "Ascribe power to God." And from this strength, God in turn bestows abundance upon this worshiper. 

Rabbi Meir ben Gabbai <note>Meir ben Ezekiel ibn Gabbai (1480–1540) was a prominent Kabbalist whose work *Avodat HaKodesh* (The Holy Service) sought to systematize Kabbalistic thought.</note>, *The Holy Service*, folio 35, columns 3 and 4.

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 241, which discussed whether the Sibylline prophecies about Christ were authentic or later Christian inventions. themselves, after the time of Christ. The Greek Sibylline verses <note>Collection of oracular utterances written in Greek hexameters ascribed to the Sibyls, prophetesses who uttered divine revelations in a frenzied state.</note> which still exist contain the aforementioned words in their initial letters; but far from revealing an ancient pagan origin, they are patched together partly from New Testament phrases and partly from verses borrowed from Greek poets. Meanwhile, this is the reason why Jesus is called **Fish**, and the fish that **Tobias** <note>A reference to the Book of Tobit in the Apocrypha, where the Archangel Raphael instructs Tobias to catch a giant fish in the Tigris River; its heart and liver were used to drive away a demon.</note> is said to have caught in the **Tigris River** and with which he drove away the devil, is also said to signify Christ or the Messiah. The occasion for calling Jesus, as the Messiah, a **Fish** also has its basis in the **Kabbalah** <term>Kabbalah: An ancient Jewish tradition of mystical interpretation of the Bible.</term> of the Jews, where the **Shekhinah** <note>original: "Schekinah"; the Hebrew term for the "dwelling" or "settling" of the divine presence of God.</note> is called a **fish pond**, and the Messiah is also called **Shekhinah** or **Dwelling**, because God dwelt in him through His works, and he corresponded to the tenth **Sephirah** <term>Sephirah: In Kabbalah, the ten emanations or attributes through which the Infinite reveals itself; the tenth Sephirah, Malkuth (Kingdom), is often associated with the Shekhinah.</term>. For the same reason, the Christian Kabbalists called **baptism** a **fish pond**;

<meta>This page continues the argument that biblical miracles should be understood as poetic or rational descriptions. It follows from the previous page’s discussion of Isaiah using the "pillar of cloud" as a metaphor for divine protection.</meta>

likened to things that are most pleasant for travelers: a cloud by day against the intense heat of the sun in the Orient <note>original: "Morgenlande"</note>, and light at night to illuminate the darkness. Why should it not be permitted for Isaiah <note>original: "Jeſaias"</note> himself to explain the miracle of the pillar of cloud and fire in the wilderness in a rational way?

Such a disruption in the entire world system <note>Weltſyſtem</note>—where the celestial bodies halted and stood still in their course, as described in the Book of Joshua <note>The biblical account in Joshua 10:12-13 where the sun and moon reportedly stopped to allow the Israelites more time for battle</note>—is quite impossible; nor is it believable that the author of this book could have meant such an absurd miracle with his narrative. The expression in the Book of Joshua, that the sun and the moon stood still, is therefore to be understood thus: Joshua won the victory, and God, through His cooperative assistance, granted him such great progress in one day that it corresponded to what is usually accomplished in twice as much time.

In the Talmud, it is also told of Moses and a later Jewish teacher named Nicodemus <note>This refers to Nakdimon ben Guryon, a figure in the Talmud known for his piety; according to tradition, God miraculously extended the daylight so he could repay a debt of water to a Roman official</note> that the sun stood still before them, just as it did before Joshua (274); and the figure of speech, *to be able to make the sun stand still in its course*, generally signifies performing incredible things; in this sense, it is said in the Talmud that one [is a] teach- <note>The text breaks off mid-word; "Leh-" likely completes as "Lehrer" (teacher).</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on interpretive methodology, transitioning from Maimonides's Jewish perspective on page 288 to the early Christian community's approach to prophecy and the historical context of scripture.</meta>

The early Christian community placed less weight on Jesus’s miracles than on prophetic pronouncements <note>original: *prophetische Aussprüche*</note> that were interpreted as referring to him; for, when encountering wondrous narratives, people did not always form the concept of actual miracles <note>original: *wirkliche Wunder*. The author suggests that the early church viewed these stories more as fulfillments of scripture than as literal supernatural disruptions of nature.</note> (297).

In order to properly understand the prophetic writings of the Jews, one must never seek within them descriptions of events that will occur in the future; rather, they should be viewed as reflections of the time in which the scripture was composed. Therefore, one must strive to obtain the most precise historical knowledge possible of that era, in the certain conviction,

---

Lactantius <note>Lucius Caecilius Firmianus Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian advisor to Constantine who sought to explain Christianity to educated Romans.</note>, *On Justice*, Book V, Chapter 3.

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 289, asserting that prophetic "prediction" in a historical context is actually a rational deduction based on observing present events and applying the principle of cause and effect.</meta>

that everything which seems to belong to a future <note>original: *Zukunft*</note> is only of such a nature that it can be regarded as an inevitable consequence of events of the present time; that the prediction <note>original: *Voraussagung*</note> of these consequences is a result of experience and of paying attention to the ordinary course of the world and of things, from which certain conclusions have been drawn that like causes produce like effects. When **Solon** <note>Solon (c. 630 – c. 560 BCE) was a famous Athenian lawmaker and poet, regarded as one of the Seven Sages of Greece.</note> remarks that the **Athenians** did not believe him when he predicted what would happen (298), no one understands this expression in any other way than that a wise man can perceive with certainty, from the indications provided by the present,

---

> (298) "And yet, I was not believed when I predicted the future." <note>original Latin: *Neque vero futura praedicenti mihi fides habebatur.*</note> Solon, in the *Letter to Epimenides* in **Diogenes Laërtius** <note>A 3rd-century biographer of Greek philosophers; his work is a primary source for the lives of ancient thinkers.</note>, Book I, Chapter 2.

<summary>This page argues that historical prophecy should be understood as rational foresight based on cause and effect rather than supernatural revelation, using the Greek sage Solon as a primary example.</summary>

This is also the reason why, in all prophetic writings, the events being foretold are regarded as those which will not only occur with certainty, but will also take place as soon as possible. The prophetic book of the **New Testament** known as the **Revelation of John** <note>Also known as the Apocalypse, the final book of the New Testament</note> is of this exact same nature; this work is so similar in character to the **Old Testament** writings that every expression appearing within it is borrowed either from those earlier books or from the Jewish interpretations <note>original: *Erklärungen der Juden*</note> of the Old Testament.
